What is Next.js?

  1. Next.js
    1. The React Framework for the Web - Vercel
      1. A meta-framework built on top of React: we still use components, props, react hooks, etc. 2. It is a framework on top of another framework
      2. Opinionated way of building React apps: Set of conventions and best practices regarding routing, data fetching etc.
        1. The conventions are given by Engineers at Vercel
          1. We can avoid boilerplate code & start building app
          2. Fixed set of rules makes building apps as a team a lot easier
          3. If everyone knows Next.js and follows the convention, then applications get built faster, fewer bugs, better developer experience
      3. Allows us to build complex full-stack web apps and sites
        1. Data fetching
        2. Data mutations on the server
      4. Allows us to use cutting-edge React features that need to be integrated into a framework: Suspense, Server Components, Server Actions, streaming, etc.
        1. Nearly impossible to integrate without a framework
        2. The pieces are part of the React team's full-stack architecture vision (requires a framework to implement)

The Next.JS Key Ingredients

  1. Server-Side Rendering (Dynamic and Static)
    1. Dynamic or static can be selected for each route
  2. File-based routing conventions (file-based convention)
    1. Folders as routes
      1. Folder name is route-name
    2. Special files for pages, layouts, loaders, errors, etc.
  3. Data fetching and mutation on the server (within React app running on a server)
    1. Fetching data directly in Server Components
    2. Mutations in Server Actions
  4. Optimizations
    1. Images
    2. Fonts
    3. SEO
    4. Preloading (automatic route preloading)

Two Flavours of Next.JS: "APP" and "PAGES" Router

  1. Modern Next.JS: "App" Router (completely different from the other)
    1. Introduced in Next.js 13.4 (2023)
    2. Recommend for new projects
      1. Implements React's full-stack architecture: Server Components, Server Actions, Streaming, etc.
        1. Available only in the modern App router
    3. Easy fetching with fetch() right in components (even in server-components)
    4. Extremely easy to construct layouts, loaders, error boundaries, etc.
    5. More advanced routing (parallel routing, intercepting routes, etc.)
    6. Better DX (Developer Experience) and UX (because of server components)
    7. Caching is very aggressive and confusing
      1. It many times does things we don't want
    8. Steep learning curve (but it's React - new React features)
      1. More capable framework
  2. Legacy Next.JS: "Pages" Router
    1. The first Next.js router since v1 (2016)
    2. Still supported and updated in the future
    3. Overall more simple and easy to learn
    4. Simple things like layouts are confusing to implement
    5. Data fetching using Next.js-specific APIs such as getStaticProps and getServerSideProps (not native React functions. They only work at page level and not at individual React component level)
  3. We're gonna learn the "app" router from the start. At the end, there is a section on the fundamentals of the "pages" router (for older Next.js project)

Setting up a Next.js Project

  1. npx create-next-app@14 the-wild-oasis-website
    1. All defaults except TS
  2. Next.JS is also a Node.JS app (uses it)
  3. Starting: npm run dev

Frequent Next.js Updates + Documentation

  1. Keeping up with framework is an essential skill for web developer

    1. Next.js documentation - must read as a professional developer
    2. to update Next.JS version:
    npm install next@latest react@latest react-dom@latest eslint-config-next@latest
    1. Next.js blog
      1. New versions are announced here

Defining Routes and Pages

  1. Each page in a react router is a page component that we export from a page.js file
  2. Custom labels for pages in VS Code:
    1. Settings
      1. custom labels:
        Add Items
        1. **/app/**/page.js: PAGE ${dirname}
  3. Conventions:
    1. page.js
    2. loading.js
    3. error.js

Navigating Between Pages

  1. Link - applies a few optimization techniques
    1. Prefetches all routes linked on a page (in production)
    2. Each page is downloaded separately as separate chunk
    3. Each page we visit is cached in the browser (stored temporarily)
      1. Not re-fetched again
  2. Next JS also provides some React hooks for programmatic navigation
    1. They don't work in page components (they are for server components)
  3. Next.js can cache pages and can serve old pages.

Constructing a Layout

  1. layout.js

    1. The root layout contains the HTML returned to client.

      1. HTML and body are mendatory
      2. Whatever is in each page is what is returned from the RootLayout
      3. The rest of the content is returned by children prop
        1. It will always be the current page
      4. Navigation part of the layout will not be re-rendered as we navigate through the application

    2. We can export page metadata from the layout

      1. Page metadata - page's title say
      export const metadata = {
  title: "The Wild Oasis",
};
      1. We can export variables - there are conventions

What are React Server Components? (RSC - Part 1)

  1. RSC - biggest and hardest in React

Why React Server Components?

  1. UI = f(state) - function of state changing over time
    1. If 100% Client-Side - As we keep updating state, the app keeps re-rendering (showing differet parts of UI and layout)
      1. We "hack" this by storing fetched data in state too
      2. Advantages:
        1. Apps are super interactive
        2. Components - re-usable packages
      3. Disadvantages:
        1. Requires lots of JS to be downloaded
          1. Devs needs to constantly think about it
        2. Client-server data waterfalls
          1. Happens when client on a page needs to fetch different data one after another
            1. When data from one component depends on data fetched in another component (large apps)
    2. 100% Server-Side
      1. PHP - no interactivity
      2. Disadvantages:
        1. No components
        2. Easy and fast to fetch all data (from data source)
        3. Close to the data source
          1. No need to build API to communicate between front-end and backend
        4. Needs to ship 0 kb of JS - faster page load for users
        5. UI = f(data)
          1. The data that determines the content of the UI
  2. UI = f(data, state) - How to achieve this?
    1. Closer to what a real-world app is
    2. How to achieve a gradient?
      1. What if we could take the best of both worlds?
        1. Having React components both on server and on client
          1. Be either interactive or be close to data source and ship on JS at all
            1. Solution: RSC

What are React Server Components?

  1. Back to pure React
    1. React Server Components (RSC)
      1. A new full-stack architecture for React apps
        1. It introduces server as a first-class citizen in React apps
      2. Introduces the server as an integral part of React component trees: server components
        1. React tree extends all the way to the server like a bridge that closes the gap between client and server
          1. By introducing Server Components
            1. RSC - name of the whole paradigm/architecture
              1. Server Component - Name of new component type introduced by RSC
                1. These components are only rendered on the server and never on the client
                  1. They fetch data on the server
                  2. They make part of UI = f(data)
                  3. Don't make it into the bundle (0 kb)
                    1. Enables us to build back-end also with React
  2. Regular components are called client components
  3. We write frontend code next to backend code in a natural way that "feels" like regular React
    1. We can also compose server and client components together
      1. Full-stack apps entirely controlled by React
  4. RSC is NOT active by default in new React apps (e.g. Vite apps): it needs to be implemented by a framework like Next.js ("app router")
    1. Any app we build in the Next.js app folder will use the new RSC architecture in which SCs become default components
      1. Default in apps that use the RSC architecture (like Next.js)
        1. Client components become opt-in
          1. Defined with "use client" directive at the top of the module

An Example + The Server-Client Boundary

  1. Sidebar, header, main elements are not interactive
    1. They could be rendered on server using SCs without shipping JS
    2. Structure:
      1. App
        1. Sidebar (not interactive)
        2. Header (not interactive)
          1. Avatar (not interactive) - can be fetched on the server & avoid client/server waterfall
          2. DarkMode (interactive) - cannot be rendered on the server
        3. Main (not interactive)
          1. Table (not interactive)
          2. CabinRow (fetches all cabin data and rendering it) (non interactive)
            1. Menu (interactive) - "use client"
              1. Duplicate (interactive) - "use client" is not required because it is already in the client-side boundary (it is under a client component)
              2. Edit (interactive)
              3. Delete (interactive)
          3. SortBy (interactive)
      2. Component tree contains both backend and frontend code (mixed tog
  2. Server-Client Boundary
    1. Split point between server and client code
    2. Client sub-tree. Child modules need no "use client"
      1. Subtrees are only executed in the browser

Server Components vs Client Components

  1. Client components
    1. "use client" needs to be used at the top of the module
    2. Only these can have state/hooks
    3. Lifting state up - possible
    4. Props - yes
    5. Data fetching - Possible, preferably with 3rd party library (React Query say)
    6. Can import (component module imports another module using import)
      1. Can import only client components (can't go back in the client-server boundary)
    7. Can render (one component calls another one - uses inside it's JSX body)
      1. Client components can render both client components and server components as long as they are passed as props
    8. When re-render?
      1. On state change (own state or parent state)
  2. Server components
    1. Default components
    2. They cannot be stateful and cannot use any hooks
    3. Lifting state up - not possible
    4. Props - Yes (must be serializable (must be convertable to a format that can be transfered) when passed to client components. No functions or classes (because they are no serializable))
    5. Data fetching - Preferred. Use async/await in component (in the top-level code, too - doesn't work in client-side component)
      1. First-class way of fetching data
      2. To pass data to client, we pass them as props
    6. Can import (component module imports another module using import)
      1. They can import both client and server components
    7. Can render (one component calls another one - uses inside it's JSX body)
      1. Can render client and server components
    8. When re-render?
      1. Yes. On URL Change (navigation)
        1. When user navigates to the URL that has a server component in question
          1. Because server components are tied to specific routes in the framework
          2. When URL changes to a route, all server components associated with the route re-render
            1. They might fetch data again

A Simple New Mental Model

  1. "Traditional" React
    1. Components -display-> View
    2. View -interaction-> updated state
    3. updated state -re-render-> component
    4. Components -fetch data-> stored state
    5. updated state -re-render-> component
  2. React with RSC
    1. Client components:
      1. Components -display-> View
      2. View -interaction-> updated state
      3. updated state -re-render-> component
      4. Components -fetch data-> stored state
      5. updated state -re-render-> component
    2. Server components
      1. Components -display-> View
      2. Components -fetch data-> to display in View / send props to client components
        1. Props are bridge across client/server boundary
      3. View -navigation (updates URL)-re-renders-> Component
        1. Triggers:
          1. Component -fetch data-> View (update)

The Good and Bad of the RSC Architecture

  1. The good:
    1. We can write React components on the front-end and on the back-end
      1. We can encapsulate server-side concerns into components as well
        1. We can compose entire full-stack apps with React components alone (+ server actions (for mutations - later))
          1. One single codebase for front and back-end
            1. API is not required in many situations
            2. Server components have more direct and secure access to the data source (no API, no exposing API keys, etc.)
              1. We can grab data from a database in a server component, and send already rendered page to browser (instead of sending only the data as json)
              2. Pure client-side app needs to worry about securely storing API keys
      2. Eliminate client-server waterfalls by fetching all the data needed for a page at once before sending it to the client (not each component)
        1. Not once piece after another
        2. Can be streamed in one-go
      3. Disappearing code: server components ship no JS, so they can import huge libraries "for free" (without increasing bundle size)
        1. Good for libraries used only for rendering data
          1. Libraries that connect to CMS
          2. Libraries for embedding things like tweets
          3. Libraries for syntax highlighting etc.
  2. The bad:
    1. Makes React more complex
      1. More things to learn and understand
        1. After understanding RSC, they are easy to use and reason about
          1. No state
          2. No effects
          3. No stale closures
          4. No memoization
    2. Things like Context API don't work in server components (like other hooks)
    3. More decisions to make: "Should this be a client or a server component?", "Should I fetch this data on the server or the client?", etc.
    4. Somtimes you still need to build an API (for example if you also have a mobile app)
      1. Not a problem with large teams
    5. Can only be used within a framework
      1. Alternative: Setting up a Vite app for it is very complex

Fetching Data in a Page

  1. Each page in a Next.js application is a server-component
    1. Pages need to be server components
      1. Navigation component is a server component (purely rendered on server)
  2. Code: app/cabins/page.js
import Navigation from "../components/Navigation";

export default async function Page() {
  // React function as asyn function is new

  const res = await fetch("https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users");
  const data = await res.json(); // data is not sent to the client, but is fetched on server and the rendered HTML page is sent back. Data is cached on client side.

  console.log(data); // prints on terminal where server is running

  return (
    <div>
      {/* <Navigation /> */}
      <h1>Cabins page</h1>

      <ul>
        {data.map((user) => (
          <li key={user.id}>{user.name}</li>
        ))}
      </ul>
    </div>
  );
}

Adding Interactivity with Client Components

  1. All components (both client and server) are first rendered on the server and then sent to the client
    1. The client components and the hydrated
  2. Code: Counter.js
"use client";

import { useState } from "react";

export default function Counter({ users }) {
  const [count, setCount] = useState(0);

  console.log(users);

  return (
    <div>
      <p>There are {users.length} users</p>
      <button onClick={() => setCount((c) => c + 1)}>{count}</button>;
    </div>
  );
}
  1. Code: app/cabins/page.js
// import { useState } from "react";
import Counter from "../components/Counter";
import Navigation from "../components/Navigation";

export default async function Page() {
  // React function as asyn function is new

  const res = await fetch("https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users");
  const data = await res.json(); // data is not sent to the client, but is fetched on server and the rendered HTML page is sent back. Data is cached on client side.

  console.log(data); // prints on terminal where server is running

  // useState(23); // Doesn't work

  return (
    <div>
      {/* <Navigation /> */}
      <h1>Cabins page</h1>

      <ul>
        {data.map((user) => (
          <li key={user.id}>{user.name}</li>
        ))}
      </ul>

      <Counter users={data} />
    </div>
  );
}

Displaying a Loading Indicator

  1. When data is loaded, it is streaming from server to the client
    1. renderToString is not used
      1. renderToReadableStream is used by Next JS
        1. Needs JS to be enabled on the browser
  2. We can stream individual components using suspense
    1. If a page had 20 components, and if only one component is fetching data, the whole page might be in loading state
      1. Solution: Suspense (later)
  3. Code: loading.js
export default function Loading() {
  // name does not matter.
  // This is global loader that works at any nesting level
  return <p>LOADING DATA</p>; // Appears while data is getting loaded and then gets swapped with the actual data
}

How RSC Works Behind the Scenes (RSC - Part 2)

A Quick Review of Rendering in React

  1. "Traditional" React (without RSC) (Skipping, rendering, diffing, reconciliation, ...)
    1. Components
    2. Tree of component instances (Component tree) -Render->
      1. Render (it is just calling component functions)
        1. Result: React element (it is JS object that contains all info necessary to construct DOM elements for the corresponding instance)
    3. React element tree ("Virtual DOM") - Commit to DOM ->
      1. Constructing DOM elements necessary to represent the different elements in React Element Tree ("Virtual DOM")
    4. DOM Elements (HTML)

How RSC Works Behind the Scenes

  1. Component Tree
    1. Server Component (SC), Client Component (CC)
    2. Steps of rendering:
      1. SCs are rendered on Server
        1. Result in React Elements
          1. They contain the output of SCs
            1. They only contain info on how the DOM for each component will look like (they don't contain the code itself to render the component)
              1. The code from SC has disappeared on the server (reason why we cannot use state, because funtions like useState and useEffect will disappear as the component is rendered)
                1. The React elements need to be sent to the client - They have to be serializable - functions are not serializable
                  1. We cannot keep state because there is no fiber-tree on the server - even if existed, it cannot be sent to the client
    3. Client Components
      1. Client components will not be rendered on the server
      2. The component tree contains a place-holder
        1. Holes where CCs will eventually be rendered
        2. Each placeholder contains:
          1. Serialized props passed from SC to CC
          2. URL to script with actual component code
            1. It is necessary because CCs can later to executed on the client
        3. A script with component code and URL pointing to it is complex
          1. It is powered by bundler the framework is using (not by React itself)
    4. RSC Payload
      1. Virtual DOM of SC + Trees of CC (un-rendered, placeholders)
      2. The data-structure is sent to the client
        1. JSON like datastructure (not exactly JSON. Data-format came up by React team by splitting data into multiple rows. Helps stream the data easily from server to client)
        2. All are strings (serialized)
        3. URLs or placeholders
    5. After the RSC payload is received by client, the client components are rendered into React elements to fill the holes
      1. Complete final VDOM exists at this point that can be comitted to the DOM.
    6. Rendering is done in two different environments. First on server, and then on client
  2. Why RSC payload? Why not render SCs as HTML?
    1. React describes UI as data, and not as finished HTML
      1. The reason why VDom and Fiber tree exist
        1. Helps React correctly react to re-renders of server components
          1. How?
            1. Whenever a SC re-renders and produces a new React Element, the Element can be merged seamlessly with the VDOM on the client
            2. Whenever SC re-renders, a new RSC payload is sent to the client
            3. React is able to merge ("reconcile") the current tree on the client with a new tree coming from the server (using the mechanisms that exist in React)
            4. As a result, UI state can be preserved when a SC re-renders, instead of completely re-generating the page as HTML
              1. If HTML is sent, the entire UI needs to be replaced with the new HTML
                1. All UI state will be lost - bad UX
                  1. RSC payload avoids this issue

Traditional React

    Component tree
        |
        render
        |
        v
    "Virtual DOM"
        |
        commit
        |
        v
    DOM Elements

React with RSC

    Component tree of SC and CC
        |
        Render SC
        |
        v
    "Virtual DOM" of SC + Component trees of CC (un-rendered) (RSC payload)
        RSC Payload - For each CC in Tree: (contains all data that React needs to render CC)
            1. "Hole" where CC will render
            2. Serialized props from SC
            3. URL to script with code
        |
        Send "RSC Payload" to client
        |
        Render CC
        |
        v
    Complete "Virtual DOM"
        |
        Commit
    DOM Elements
    
1. Steps **don't wait** for one another (not sequential and blocking). Completed render work (on server) is **streamed** to client (and integrated seamlessly onto the tree on the client)
    1. More fluid and faster for the user
2. UI = f(data, state) - not 100% correct
    1. UI = f(data)(state) (data is input of the hypothetical function, and state is the input to the function returned by the hypothetical function)

RSC vs. SSR: How are They Related? (RSC - Part 3)

  1. SSR & RSC are not the same thing
    1. They interact with each other

Review: Server-Side Rendering (SSR)

  1. We'll be talking about dynamic SSR (HTML generated at runtime)

  2. Process:

     Server					Client
 
 Component tree -JS---+> Hydrated (adds interactivity to HTML)
     |                |		|
     render           |		v
     |                |	Interactive React App (DOM is generated on the server)
     v                |
 HTML -------shipped--+
    1. SSR: "Just take this component tree, render it as HTML, and send that HTML to the browser"
    2. Also send the React code to make the HTML interactive

The Relationship Between SRC and SSR

  1. Process:

     Server					Client
 
 What about RSC?
    1. RSC vs. SSR
      1. RSC is NOT the same as SSR: they are separate technologies
      2. RSC does NOT replace SSR
        1. To complement it (to work with it)
      3. They usually work together:
        1. Frameworks can combine them
          1. Next.JS
      4. SSR works when it is coupled with RSC. ALL components are pre-rendered on the server
        1. SSR: "Just take this component tree, render it as HTML, and send that HTML to the browser"
      5. Both client and server components are initially rendered on the server when SSR is used

  2. Rendering process with Next.JS:

     Component tree of SC and CC
     |
     Render SC
     |
     v
 "Virtual DOM" of SC + Component trees of CC (un-rendered) (RSC payload)
     RSC Payload - For each CC in Tree: (contains all data that React needs to render CC)
         1. "Hole" where CC will render
         2. Serialized props from SC
         3. URL to script with code
     |
     Send "RSC Payload" to client
     |
     Render CC
     |
     v
 Complete "Virtual DOM"
     |
     Commit
 DOM Elements
    1. React Server & React Client
      1. They are just two different environment on the Server
        1. Two different parts of RSC protocol
      2. React Server
        1. It doesn't need to be an actual web-server. Simply "another computer"
    2. Result: RSC does NOT require a running web server! Components could run only once at build time (static site generation)
      1. Developer can read files from the file-system during development process (on own computer)
    3. React Client:
      1. It doesn't need to be a traditional web-browser
      2. It is only the part of the protocol that consumes the rendered react app
        1. Consuming - To render the app as HTML, instead of DOM elements
          1. SSR takes the entire component tree and renders it as HTML & sends to browser
            1. Both server components and client components are rendered on the server that Next.JS provides in the two environments on the React server components protocol
              1. React Server
              2. React Client
    4. Both React Client and React Server environments run on the server on initial render
      1. Output is HTML (not DOM nodes)
        1. Sent to browser
      2. Also works like this when coupled with RSC. ALL components are pre-rendered on the server
    5. Simplified process:

      1. It is similar to the initial diagram

         Component tree (SC and CC)--JS-+--> Hydrate
     |                          |		|
     render -- RSC Payload -----+		v
     |                          |	Interactive React App
     v                          |
 HTML --------------------------+
        1. JS - many different chunks (code-splitting and streaming is used)
        2. RSC Payload - Sent with JS so that React has the entire component tree on the client, not just HTML. Necessary to preserve UI state on future SC re-renders
        3. Only CCs are interactive
        4. One React app is on Client following the RSC architecture

      2. SSR happens only on initial render. On re-renders, client components only render on the actual client

        1. React server is then just a web-server where Next.JS is running
        2. React client is then just the web-browser

      3. When SC re-renders, a new RSC payload is generated and sent to the client

        1. To preserve the existing UI state

  3. Take-aways

    1. Both SCs and CCs are rendered on the server on the initial SSR
    2. After that, SCs run on web-server, and CCs run on web-browser

Section 33: Starting to Build the "Wild Oasis" Website

Section Overview

  1. Topics:
    1. Next.js project structure
      1. Clean structure
      2. Different pages
    2. Implementing pages
    3. Font and image optimization techniques
    4. Adding metadata and favicon

Project Planning: "The Wild Oasis" Customer Website

The Project: The Wild Oasis Website

  1. Remember: "The Wild Oasis" is a small boutique hotel with 8 luxurious wooden cabins
    1. We built their application to manage everything about the hotel: bookings, cabins, and guests
    2. We also build the API using Supabase
    3. Now they need a customer-facing website where guests can learn about the hotel, browse all cabins, reserve a cabin, and define and update their profile (before even booking or checking into hotel)
    4. Updating data in the internal app should update the website, so we use the same DB and API

Project Requirements From the Business

  1. Users of the app are potenial guests and actual guests (those who planned reservation)
  2. Guests should be able to learn all about the Wild Oasis Hotel - About
  3. Guests should be able to get information about each cabin and see booked dates - Cabins
  4. Guests should be able to filter the cabins by their maximum guest capacity - Cabins
    1. For perfect fit for themselves
  5. Guests should be able to reserve a cabin for a certain date range - Reservations
  6. Reservations are not paid online. Payments will be made at the property upon arrival. Therefore, new reservations should be set to "unconfirmed" (booked but not yet checked in) - Reservations
  7. Guests should be able to view all their past and future reservations - Reservations
  8. Guests should be able to update or delete a reservation - Reservations
  9. Guests need to sign up and log in before they can reserve a cabin and perform any operation (delete or update reservation) - Authentication
  10. On sign up, each guest should get a profile in the DB - Authentication
  11. Guests should be able to set and update basic data about their profile to make check-in at the hotel faster - Profile

Features + Pages

  1. Homepage - /
  2. About
    1. About page: /about
  3. Cabins
    1. Cabin overview: /cabins/
    2. Cabin detail: /cabins/:cabinId
  4. Reservations
    1. Cabin detail - used for reservation
    2. Reservation list - /account/reservations
    3. Edit reservation - /account/reservation/edit
  5. Authentication
    1. Login - /login (and sign-up)
  6. Profile
    1. Update profile - /account/profile

Technology Decisions

  1. Framework - Next.JS
    1. The most popular React meta-framework. Handles routing, SSR, data fetching and even remote state management (in a way...), therefore replacing many tools we had to include before
      1. Less decisions to take
        1. React Router is not required
        2. React Query is not required
  2. UI State management - Context API
    1. We might still need global UI state in Next.js app. For that, we can use the Context API, Redux, or any of the other solutions. In this case the Context API will be enough.
  3. DB/API - supabase
    1. We'll use the data and API we already built in the first "Wild Oasis" project. If you skipped that project, please go back to the "Supabase" section to set everything up
  4. Styling - tailwindcss
    1. Modern way of writing CSS. Extremely easy to integrate into Next.js. Most styles and markup will be pre-written anyway in this project.
      1. Tailwind is automatically set up by Next.js starter

Project Organization

  1. _components - opt out of routing
    1. No router is created for the folder

Styling with Tailwind CSS

  1. With fresh install, we get globals.css with the following content:
@tailwind base;
@tailwind components;
@tailwind utilities;

  1. Tailwind:

    1. Utility classes
    2. Predefined colors
    3. Predefined values
      1. Good values for good design

  3. Styled Components - supported by Next.js (tailwind is more modern)

  4. Adding icons:

npm i @heroicons/react

Adding Page Metadata and Favicon

  1. export const metadata - Next.js convention
    1. The constant with title becomes the title of the HTML
    2. All metadata should be defined in metadata constant
    3. meatadata exported from a page overrides the global metadata defined in layout.js
  2. Placing favicon:
    1. Just place favicon.ico or just an image file called icon in the app folder
      1. app/icon.png (or any image type)
        1. <link rel="icon" href="/icon.png?sga798a7sgsdf09sd" type="image/png" sizes="163x163">
          1. Automatically sized

Loading and Optimization Fonts

  1. Fonts performance optimization
    1. Automatically self-host Google fonts, without the font being downloaded from Google
      1. It will prevent layout shifts, improve performance, and privacy
        1. If we use fonts from Google fonts, it might be a problem with privacy. It may not be good for GDBR regulation.
        2. May not be good for performance, because the font files may need to be downloaded from a Google server.
          1. Best to have the files on own server
            1. Downloading will be faster
            2. Prevents layout shifts
  2. Code: layout.js

Optimizing Images with Next.js Component

  1. Images - Images are one of the biggest factors contributing to page-size
    1. Huge impact on loading speeds
  2. Next.js - Image component - produces optimized image
    1. It will automatically server correct size images in modern format (webp)
      1. It does it only on demand (when necessary)
        1. srcset is used - multiple images for different screen sizes
    2. Prevents layout shifts
      1. Forces us to specify height and width
    3. Automatically lazy-loads, only when they enter the viewport
      1. Complicated to implement manually
      2. loading="lazy"
  3. Image - it can be used in many different ways. The above is only one way
    1. If we import image first, height and width are not required (original size is retained). We can also change the quality

What is React Suspense?

  1. Suspense

    1. Built-in React component that we can use to catch/isolate components (or entire subtrees) that are not ready to be rendered ("suspending")

      1. They are doing async work
      2. The components or subtrees are suspending
      3. It is like a catch block in a try-catch statement
        1. Instead of catching errors, it catches components that are suspending

    2. What causes a component to be suspending?

      1. Fetching data (with a spported library - that supports suspense)
      2. Loading code (with React's lazy loading feature)
      3. Use-case of suspense - data-fetching
        1. Data-fetching libraries like React-Query or Next.js support suspense

    3. Example:

       App
     Nav
     Suspense (Built-in React component - to wrap Products component)
         Products (component in Next.js that will fetch data (will be suspending - while it fetches the product data)
             List
         Filter
      1. Native way to support asynchronous operations in a declarative way (no more isLoading states and render logic)
        1. Declarative - using components instead of imperative render logic
          1. Makes components simpler and cleaner

    4. Steps:

      1. While rendering, suspending component is found (Product in this case)
      2. Go to closest Suspense parent ("boundary") and discard already rendered children
        1. It separates the suspending subtree from the rest of the app
      3. Display fallback component/JSX
        1. While async operation is happening in the background (usually a spinner)
        2. After async work is done
      4. Render subtree under suspense boundary (with the product data in this case)

    5. Important: Components do NOT automatically suspend just because an async operation is happening inside them. Integrating async operations with Suspense is hard, so we use libraries (React Query, Next.js, Remix, etc.)

      1. It is extremely complex and integrating async operations with suspense is very hard
      2. Works with React.Lazy

A Look Behind the Scenes

  1. Fiber Tree Representation

     App
 |
 child
 |
 Nav -Sibling- Suspense
               |
               Products ----- Filter
               |
               List
    1. Fiber tree - Internal representation of each component instance and DOM element of our app
      1. Comparing with VDom - Fiber tree is a mutable data-structure that is never destroyed (perfect place to store state, hooks, ...)
      2. It enables concurrent features
        1. Allows React pausing rendering with suspense
      3. Reconciliation phase:
        1. Behind the scenes, the child tree below the Suspense component is moved into another built-in component called Activity (available only in fiber-tree)
        2. The fallback Spinner is placed as a sibling to the Activity
          1. Fallback is initially hidden.
            1. Because, mode on Activity is set to Visible (Suspense children are visible) (Activity = 'Visible')
        3. When component (Products) suspends, the mode is set to Hidden (Activity = 'Hidden')
          1. Fallback (Spinner) will be rendered, and the subtree will be hidden
        4. When the async task finishes, Activity is set to Visible and the final subtree gets rendered
        5. If the component gets suspended againt, the fallback gets rendered again and the process gets repeated
          1. The subtree is always kept in the fiber-tree (elements are not destroyed)
            1. Consequences - All state is preserved in subsequent suspending and unsuspending phases
              1. Constraint - the fallback will be rendered if the component suspends again
                1. Fallback will NOT be shown again if the Suspense trigger is wrapped in a transition (React's startTransition function). In Next.js, that's the case with page navigations (all page navigations are wrapped in transitions. If a component refetches data as a result of page transition, fallback will not rendered again (not what we want)). We can reset the Suspense boundary with a unique key prop (resets suspense boundary)
      4. How does Suspense actually know that a component is suspending? (it's child component)

        1. Behind the scenes, the suspended component throws a Promise

           throw new Promise(...)
          1. This triggers the suspense boundary to render the fallback
          2. This is a notification to the closest suspense boundary

  2. Fiber tree with during reconciliation

     App
 |
 child
 |
 Nav -Sibling- Suspense
               |     (`Activity = 'Visible'|'Hidden'`)
               Activity ----- Spinner ('Hidden' - rendered)
               | ('Visible' - rendered)
               Products ----- Filter
               |
               List

Streaming UI with Suspense: Cabin List

  1. Rule: Move data fetching as close to the component as possible which needs it
    1. For more granular data-fetching strategy
  2. Suspense must be outside the component that does the asynchronous work (CabinList is async component)
  3. Code: cabins/page.js
import { Suspense } from "react";

export async function generateMetadata({ params }) {
  // Next.js waits for the following function to complete before streaming the UI to the client. Head tags are streamed first
  const { name } = await getCabin(params.cabinId);

  };

Error Handling: Setting Up Error Boundaries

  1. error.js - we can have nested error boundaries
    1. Works only in rendering. Callback function errors will not be caught by error boundary
    2. Might not catch errors in the root layout (layout.js)
      1. global-error.js
        1. Replaces the entire layout (even the root layout)
          1. It needs its own HTML and Body tags
        2. Nest.js documentation has details about conventions
  2. Code: error.js
"use client";

export default function Error({ error, reset }) {
  return (
    <main className="flex justify-center items-center flex-col gap-6">
      <h1 className="text-3xl font-semibold">Something went wrong!</h1>
      <p className="text-lg">{error.message}</p>

      <button
        className="inline-block bg-accent-500 text-primary-800 px-6 py-3 text-lg"
        onClick={reset}
      >
        Try again
      </button>
    </main>
  );
}

import { eachDayOfInterval } from "date-fns";
import { supabase } from "./supabase";
import { notFound } from "next/navigation";

/////////////
// GET

export async function getCabin(id) {
  const { data, error } = await supabase
    .from("cabins")
    .select("*")
    .eq("id", id)
    .single();

  // For testing
  // await new Promise((res) => setTimeout(res, 1000));

  if (error) {
    console.error(error);
    notFound(); // navigates to the error page
  }

  return data;
}

Different Types of SSR: Static vs. Dynamic Rendering

Server-Side Rendering in Next.JS

  1. Next.js is a React framework, so rendering is done by React, following the rules we learned earlier
  2. Remember: Both Server and Client components are rendered on the server on the initial render
  3. In Next.js, the server-side rendering work is split by routes
    1. Uses React and React DOM libraries to render each route one by one on the server
      1. Each route can be either static (also called pre-rendered), or dynamic
        1. It is not the entire app that is rendered statically or dynamically. It is each route individually
  4. There is also Partial Pre-Rendering (PPR) which mixes dynamic and static rendering in the same route (more later)

Static vs Dynamic Rendering

  1. Static Rendering
    1. HTML is generated at build time (developer triggers the rendering), or periodically in the background by re-fetching data (ISR, more on this later)
      1. ISR - Incremental Static Regeneration (periodic rendering)
    2. Useful when data doesn't change often and is not personolized to user (e.g. product page)
      1. Product page doesn't need any data that belongs to or generated by the user
    3. Default rendering strategy in Next.js (even when a page or component fetches data)
    4. When deployed to Vercel, each static route is automatically hosted on a CDN (company that built Next.js farmework)
    5. If all routes of an app are static, the entire app can be exported as a static site (SSG)
  2. Dynamic Rendering
    1. HTML is generated at request time (a new version for each new request that reaches the server) - User triggers the rendering
    2. Makes sense if:
      1. The data changes frequently and is personalized to the user (e.g. shopping cart - each user has a different shopping cart)
      2. Rendering a route requires information that depends on request (e.g. search params from URL, cookie, header, etc.)
    3. A route automatically switches to dynammic rendering in certain conditions (next slide)
      1. Static pages are way faster (don't have to be rebuilt for every request)
    4. When deployed to Vercel, each dynamic route becomes a serverless function

When Next.JS Switches to Dynamic Rendering

  1. Usually, developers don't directly choose whether a route should be static or dynamic. Next.js will automatically switch to dynamic rendering in the following scenarios:
    1. The route has a dynamic segment (page uses params)
      1. They can be known only at request time
    2. searchParams (query params from URL) are used in the page component
      1. /product?quantity=123
    3. Incoming headers() or cookies() are used in any of the route's server components
    4. An uncached data request is made in any of the route's server components
      1. We can influence the caching behavior
  2. This is necessary because any of the values can not be known by Next.js at build time
    1. Route must be rendered anew for each request
  3. We can also force Next.js to render a route dynamically:
    1. export const dynamic = 'force-dynamic'; from page.js
    2. export const revalidate = 0; from page.js
    3. { cache: 'no-store' } added to a fetch request in any of the route's server components
    4. noStore() in any of the route's server components

Some Terminology You Might Need

  1. Content Delivery Network (CDN): A network of servers located around the globe that cache and deliver a website's static content (HTML, CSS, JS, images) from as close as possible to each user.
    1. Data doesn't need to travel the entire planet from the host to the user
    2. Hosting providers like: Vercel, Netlify, Render.com will host static assets on global CDN
  2. Serverless Computing: With the serverless computing model, we can run application code, usually back-end code, without managing the server ourselves. Instead, we can just run single functions on a cloud provider (AWS, Vercel, ...): serverless functions. The server in initialized and active only for the duration the serverless function is running, unlike a traditional Node.js app where the server is constantly running. Remember: each dynamic route becomes a serverless function
    1. Each dynamic route becomes a serverless function on vercel
    2. Next.js is not a Node.js app always running in Vercel
    3. If there is huge traffic to a serverless function, Vercel will automatically allocate more resources to handle the additional load
    4. Static content will be generated during build-time and hosted on a CDN
  3. The "edge": "As close as possible to the user". A CDN is part of an "edge" network, but there is also serverless "edge" computing. This is serverless computing that does not happen on a central server, but on a network that's distributed around the globe, as close as possible to the user (like a CDN but for running code). Important: we can select certain routes to run on the edge when deployed to Vercel.
    1. They become faster
  4. Incremental Static Regenaration (ISR): A Next.js feature that allows developers to update the content of a static page, in the background, even after the website has already been built and deployed. This happens by re-fetching the data of a component or entire route after a certain interval (we define in code). More on this later.

Making Dynamic Pages Static with generateStaticParams

  1. If we tell Next.js the set of IDs we know already, then Next.js can render the pages statically
    1. Then the entire site is a static site, and we can host on any static hostic provider
  2. Code: cabins/[cabinId]/page.js
export async function generateStaticParams() {
  const cabins = await getCabins();

  const ids = cabins.map((cabin) => {
    cabinId: String(cabin.id);
  }); // `cabinId` is the name of the folder

  return ids;
}

Partial Pre-Rendering

Partial Pre-Rendering (PPR): A Whole New Way of Rendering

  1. Idea/problem: Most pages don't need to be 100% static or 100% dynamic
    1. Example: If the route is static but displays logged in user name
      1. If the route is rendered dynamically, it is waste
        1. Most of the route except the dynamic part can be rendered at build-time and cached in CDN
          1. Solution: PPR
  2. New rendering strategy that combines static and dynamic rendering in the same route (it is between fully static and fully dynamic)
  3. How does it work?
    1. A static (pre-rendered) shell is served immediately from a CDN, leaving holes for dynamic content
      1. Whenever user visits the page
    2. Server in the meantime renders dynamic content (which takes time as compared to the static shell)
      1. The slower dynamic content is streamed in as it's rendered on the server
        1. Filling the holes
  4. Result: Even faster pages that can mostly be delivered from the edge (CDN) even when there are small dynamically rendered parts on the page
    1. Example: 80% static content, 20% dynamic content
      1. Using Partial Prerendering

How to Use Partial Pre-Rendering

  1. As of Next.js 14, PPR is highly experimental and should not be used in production
    1. PPR needs to be turned on in config file
      1. Static rendering is default rendering mechanism
      2. If PPR is enabled, by default, as much as possible of any route will be statically rendered, constructing a static shell
        1. Dynamic parts (components) should be placed inside Suspense boundaries
    2. There are no new APIs to learn for PPR
      1. These boundaries tell Next.js that anything within the boundary is dynamic
        1. The boundary prevents the dynamic part (e.g. reading a header or making a non-cached fetch request) from spreading onto the entire route
          1. Suspense boundary isolates dynamic components or subtrees that will be dynamically rendered
      2. We provide a static fallback to be shown while the dynamic part is rendered (by passing it to the suspense component)
        1. Dynamic components or sub-trees are inserted into the static shell as they become available

How Next.js Caches Data

  1. Caching in Next.JS
    1. Next.JS Caching
      1. Caching: Storing fetched or computed data in a temporary location for future access, instead of having to re-fetch or re-compute the data every time it's needed
      2. Next.js caches very aggressively: everything that is possible to cache, is cached
        1. Fetched data
        2. Visited routes
      3. Next.js provides APIs for cache revalidation: removing data from the cache and updating it with fresh data (re-fetched or re-computed)
      4. Makes Next.js apps more performant and saves costs (computing and data access costs)
        1. If paying each time we access data using an API (if we fetch less often, we pay much less)
        2. Problem: Aggressive caching
          1. Caching always ON by default: strange and unexpected behavior in some situations (page displaying stale data on the client). Some caches can't be turned off (annoying)
      5. Very confusing: Many different Next.js APIs effect and control caching (difficult to grasp especially for the beginners)
  2. Topics:
    1. 4 Caching Mechanisms

The Caching Mechanisms

  1. Request Memoization (Server)
    1. What data?
      1. Data fetched with similar GET requests (same url and options in fetch function)
    2. How long?
      1. One page request (one render, by one user)
        1. Data is cached and reused exactly during one page render
          1. When a route fetches the same data in multiple places in the component tree during one render, only one Network request is made
    3. Enables
      1. No need to fetch at the top of tree: the same fetch in multiple components only makes one request
        1. Example: If we fetch products from 5 different components, Next.js gets the data from the API once, and not 5 times
    4. Works only with native fetch function and when the requests are exactly the same (same URL and options object)
    5. It is a React feature
      1. Works only in React component tree
        1. Not in route handlers or server actions
    6. How to revalidate?
      1. N.A. - data is not persisted anywhere
    7. How to opt out?
      1. AbordController with fetch function
  2. Data Cache (Server)
    1. What data?
      1. Data fetched in a route or a single fetch request
    2. How long?
      1. Indefinietely, even acress re-deploys (can revalidate (clear and re-fetch) or opt out)
        1. Data is available across multiple requests from different users
          1. If there are million users sending the same request, Next.js sends only one Network request (every user gets the exact same data - static data - data that feeds into static pages - data used to statically render routes)
    3. Enables
      1. Data for static pages + ISR when revalidated (the whole page is re-generated on revalidation)
        1. ISR is possible with this cache and revalidation of the cache
    4. Advantages:
      1. Prevents too many requests to original data source
      2. Most configurable
    5. Disadvantages:
      1. Most confusing
    6. How to revalidate? (revalidating Data Cache also revalidates Full Route Cache)
      1. Time-based (automatic) for all data on page:
        1. export const revalidate = <time>; (page.js)
          1. Data will be re-fetched
      2. Time-based (automatic) for one data request: (revalidating only a certain fetch request)
        1. fetch('...', { next: { revalidate: <time> } })
      3. On-demand (manual):
        1. revalidatePath or revalidateTag
          1. Called to revalidate on a certain path or certain tag (?)
    7. How to opt out? (The below options force page to become dynamic, which also opts our of the Full Route Cache)
      1. Entire page:
        1. export const revalidate = 0; (page.js) (always re-validate)
          1. Turns the cache off
      2. Entire page:
        1. export const dynamic = 'force-dynamic'; (page.js)
          1. Turns off the data cache (since the cache is only for static data)
      3. Individual request:
        1. fetch('...', { cache: 'no-store' })
      4. Individual server component: noStore() (calling it inside of it)
  3. Full Route Cache (Server)
    1. What data?
      1. Entire static pages (HTML and RSC payload) (full route cache)
        1. At build-time
    2. How long?
      1. Until the "Data cache" is invalidated (or app is re-deployed)
        1. If underlying data changes, the page needs to be re-gerenerated and stored in the cache again to reflect the latest data
        2. Cleared when new version of the application is deployed
    3. Enables
      1. Static pages
        1. Storage mechanism for static routes
  4. Router Cache (Client)
    1. What data?
      1. Pre-fetched and visited pages (while navigating around the application): static and dynamic [routes] (in Browser. The browser doesn't know how the route was generated at all)
        1. Idea: Pages stored in memory allows almost instance access (gives the use the feel of a single page application with no hard reloads)
    2. 30 sec dynamic / 5 min static (throughout one user session)
    3. Enables
      1. SPA-like navigation (instant navigation and no full reloads)
        1. Pages are not requested from the server again as user navigates back and forth (results in stale data being displayed on the page)
          1. Dynamic pages - 30 seconds
          2. Static pages - 5 minutes
      2. There is no way to revalidate the cache
        1. Unless user performs hard reload/closes and re-opens
    4. How to revalidate?
      1. revalidatePath or revalidateTag in SA - manually revalidating data cache
        1. SA - Server Action (later)
        2. Intutition: If we declare the data in cache as old (by revalidating it), the pages that are cache in user's browser that depend on the data should be refreshed with fresh data
      2. router.refresh - forcing reload
      3. cookie.set or cookie.delete in SA
    5. How to opt out?
      1. Not possible (makes no sense)
        1. Can be problematic - Users always want up to date date. We cannot guarantee that with this cache
        2. If data on a page is updated by other than revalidation, (without cache knowing about the udate), we cannot do anything about it
          1. Example: If vanilla React app updates data
  5. This is the behavior in production mode. Caching doesn't work in development (we always get fresh data while building the apps)

Experimenting with Caching and ISR

  1. Caching is disabled in Dev environment

    1. Need to simulate prod environment

       npm run build
 npm start

      1. Script:

         "prod": "next build && next start"

    2. npm run prod

      1. The site is rendered as static site

    3. If data is changed in DB, the data doesn't get refreshed on the page

      1. That is because of data cache and full route cache
        1. Route is cached with the data that was in place when the page was statically generated (this will be used until we re-validate the data)
      2. We don't have to go to DB if we don't have to query from db again
  2. We want middle ground
    1. Incremental Static Regeneration

      1. Re-generates static page and fetch fresh data from time to time

         export const revalidate = <number-of-seconds>; // regerated after every <number-of-seconds>

      2. If the cabin prices change once per day, we can set it to once per hour. Once per minute makes sure maximum for 1 minute we have stale data

        1. If user wants to book the cabin, then we want the fresh data
        2. It is the first user who visits the page after a value has changed, will regenerate the page in the background for the next user (next visitor will get the result)
  3. We can use revalidation at the component level or at the individual fetch level

Section 35: Client and Server Interactions

Section Overview

  1. How client and server portions of Next.js interact with each other
  2. Topics:
    1. Composing full-stack apps
    2. Rendering server components in client components
      1. When it makes sense
    3. Sharing state between server and client
      1. In both directions
        1. URL
        2. Context API
    4. URL and React Context API
    5. How to think about data fetching
      1. How and where to fetch and stream different pieces of related data on a page

Blurring the Boundary Between Server and Client (RSC - Part 4)

The Server-Client Boundary: Front-End vs. Back-End

  1. Traditional

     Back-End [API] (Node.JS)
 ^			|
 |			|
 Mutations 	JSON DATA [GET]
 | [POST],	|
 | [PUT]		|
 |			v
 Front-End (React)
    1. They may have different code-bases
    2. They might be hosted on different platforms
      1. Very clear server-client boundary
    3. Communication happens via an API
    4. Once JSON arrives from the back-end, the front-end takes over (completely)
      1. Backend is not involved in rendering the data

  2. Next.JS with RSC + SA

                 [SC] (Data: Render directly)
             |
 +-----------+-------+
 |					|
 [CC] (front-end)	[SC] (back-end)
 |					|
 |					+-----------+
 |					|			|
 [SC]				[SC] <-+	[CC] (Data: Send as props) (no need of API)
                     |      SA (Mutations: Server Actions)
                     [CC] --+
    1. No clear separation between front-end and back-end anymore
      1. Lines are completely blurred (lines are flexible and dynamic)
    2. "Knitting": pieces of server and client code interweave (composability)
      1. Powerful mechanism to compose server & client into full-stack app with single code-base
    3. Allow us to build true full-stack applications in just one codebase
      1. Seperation is not always good
      2. No longer need to build API
    4. No need for an intermediary API many times
    5. SA - replaces POST, PUT, DELETE, ...

Importing vs. Rendering

  1. Component tree:

                 [SC]
             |
 +-----------+-------+
 |					|
 [CC] (front-end)	[SC] (back-end)
 |					|
 |					+-----------+
 |					|			|
 [SC]				[CC] 		[CC]
                     |     
                     [CC]
    1. A parent component renders a child component that it is connected to
    2. A client component renders a server component that is is connected to (how?)
      1. Important trick

        1. Possible if server component is passed as prop (children or other regular prop)

          1. Example:
          import B from 'B';
import C from 'C';
import D from 'D';

function A() {
    return (
        <div>
            <B>
                <D>Hey </D>
            </B>
        </div>
    );
}

        2. D has already been executed here... (on the server)

          1. Component A imports D too

            1. Dependency tree: (modules imported by other modules)

               A (SC)
 |
 +-----------+-----------+
 |			|			|
 B (CC)		D (SC)		C (SC)
                         |
                         +-------+
                         |		|
                         E (CC)	E (CC)
                         |
                         C (CC)
              1. A imports D because or else, it cannot be passed as a prop
              2. In the dependency tree, the client/server boundary is established (not in the component tree
                1. Client components cannot import server components but only client components
                  1. It is not possible to go back over the client/server boundary and import server components from the client
                    1. Client components can render server components as long as they have been passed as props
              3. In the example:
                1. A imports both B and D
                2. A executes D, before passing the result as prop to B (client component)
                  1. Instance of D is already created on server and then passed as prop to client
              4. Server components can import an render all components

  2. A component can be both SC and CC

    1. A component is just a definition (a blueprint) and can be used for creation of multiple component instances

      1. If no "use client" directive, it becomes SC instance as soon as it is used
        1. Example: When A has imported C, it becomes an SC instance

    2. If a client component imports a server component, a CC instance is created

       E (CC) "use client"
 |
 C (CC)
      1. C is imported by E (CC), so C becomes a CC

    3. If SC is imported and used in CC, a CC instance is created

Client Components in Server Components

  1. Using CC in SC
    1. If there is a long text, and if we want a button to expand the text on demand
      1. Tip: We have to move a CC as low as possible in the component tree
        1. Because, all child components of a CC are CCs (they will be rendered on a client)
          1. Leaves are better
  2. Code: TextExpander.js
"use client";

import Logo from "./Logo";

...

<Logo /> {/* This becomes a CC instance even though it is defined as SC */}
  1. hydrateRoot()
    1. Component is first rendered on the server & then hydrated
  2. Suspense
    1. Suspended: true - shows loading spinner

Highlighting Current Side Navigation Link

  1. Code: SideNavigation.js
"use client";

import {
  CalendarDaysIcon,
  HomeIcon,
  UserIcon,
} from "@heroicons/react/24/solid";
import SignOutButton from "@/app/_components/SignOutButton";
import Link from "next/link";
import { usePathname } from "next/navigation";

const navLinks = [
  {
    name: "Home",
    href: "/account",
    icon: <HomeIcon className="h-5 w-5 text-primary-600" />,
  },
  {
    name: "Reservations",
    href: "/account/reservations",
    icon: <CalendarDaysIcon className="h-5 w-5 text-primary-600" />,
  },
  {
    name: "Guest profile",
    href: "/account/profile",
    icon: <UserIcon className="h-5 w-5 text-primary-600" />,
  },
];

function SideNavigation() {
  const pathName = usePathname(); // Works only in client components. The component is switched to CC to use React hook.

  return (
    <nav className="border-r border-primary-900">
      <ul className="flex flex-col gap-2 h-full text-lg">
        {navLinks.map((link) => (
          <li key={link.name}>
            <Link
              className={`py-3 px-5 hover:bg-primary-900 hover:text-primary-100 transition-colors flex items-center gap-4 font-semibold text-primary-200 ${
                pathName === link.href ? "bg-primary-900" : ""
              }`}
              href={link.href}
            >
              {link.icon}
              <span>{link.name}</span>
            </Link>
          </li>
        ))}

        <li className="mt-auto">
          <SignOutButton />
        </li>
      </ul>
    </nav>
  );
}

export default SideNavigation;

Sharing State Between Client and Server: The URL

  1. How to pass data from client to server?

  2. Filter component - to filter by cabin capacity

    1. How to share state from client to server
      1. Store state in URL
      2. In SC, we can read the data from the URL
        1. searchParams is only available in the page.js (not in SCs)
          1. searchParams can only be known at the runtime - The page cannot be statically rendered (dynamically rendered)
            1. revalidate doesn't work

  3. A navigation (new URL) in Next.js is wrapped in a React transition

    1. In transition, Suspense will not hide the content that has already been rendered earlier
      1. Next.js will wait and swap it out as new content comes in

        1. Fix: Pass in a unique key in Suspense

           <Suspense fallback={<Spinner />} key={filter}> {/* Because `filter` is unique */}
     ...
 </Suspense>

  4. Recap:

    1. Sharing state from client to server
      1. Place it in the URL
        1. Makes pages sharable and bookmarkable
    2. router.replace - used to navigate to the new URL
    3. Whenever searchParams changes (as a result of URL changing), the server component will re-render (when there is navigation)
      1. As a result, child components also re-render (just like in client-side React)
        1. Re-fetching data might occur
    4. Suspense needs a unique key to show fallback

  5. Code: Filter.js

"use client";

import { usePathname, useRouter, useSearchParams } from "next/navigation";

function Filter() {
  const searchParams = useSearchParams();
  const router = useRouter();
  const pathname = usePathname();

  const activeFilter = searchParams.get("capacity") ?? "all";

  function handleFilter(filter) {
    const params = new URLSearchParams(searchParams);
    params.set("capacity", filter); // doesn't change URL bar
    router.replace(`${pathname}?${params.toString()}`, { scroll: false }); // doesn't allow page to scroll to the top
  }

  return (
    <div className="border border-primary-800 flex">
      <Button
        filter="all"
        handleFilter={handleFilter}
        activeFilter={activeFilter}
      >
        All cabins
      </Button>
      <Button
        filter="small"
        handleFilter={handleFilter}
        activeFilter={activeFilter}
      >
        1&mdash;3 guests
      </Button>
      <Button
        filter="medium"
        handleFilter={handleFilter}
        activeFilter={activeFilter}
      >
        4&mdash;7 guests
      </Button>
      <Button
        filter="large"
        handleFilter={handleFilter}
        activeFilter={activeFilter}
      >
        8&mdash;12 guests
      </Button>
    </div>
  );
}

function Button({ filter, handleFilter, activeFilter, children }) {
  // also becomes a client component
  return (
    <button
      className={`px-5 py-2 hover:bg-primary-700 ${
        filter === activeFilter ? "bg-primary-700 text-primary-50" : ""
      }`}
      onClick={() => handleFilter(filter)}
    >
      {children}
    </button>
  );
}

export default Filter;

Data Fetching Strategies for the Reservation Section

  1. Reservation on each cabin page
    1. We need to fetch data
      1. We can do it in multiple pieces of data in multiple places
        1. Different strategies - on a single page
  2. Clients should only be passed minimum amount of data (only the required amount of data)
  3. We need to think about data fetching strategies when building real applications
  4. Code: Cabin.js
import Image from "next/image";

              Located in the heart of the{" "}

            </span>

            <EyeSlashIcon className="h-5 w-5 text-primary-600" />
            <span className="text-lg">
              Privacy <span className="font-bold">100%</span> guaranteed
            </span>
          </li>

      </div>
    </div>
  );

export async function generateMetadata({ params }) {
  // Next.js waits for the following function to complete before streaming the UI to the client. Head tags are streamed first
  const { name } = await getCabin(params.cabinId);
  return {
    title: `Cabin ${name}`,
  };
}

export async function generateStaticParams() {
  const cabins = await getCabins();

  const ids = cabins.map((cabin) => {
    cabinId: String(cabin.id);
  }); // `cabinId` is the name of the folder

  return !ids || ids.length === 0 ? [{ cabinId: "not-found" }] : ids;
}

Using the Context API for State Management

  1. Change URL for each data range selection would cause a new navigation

    1. Whenever we navigate to a different page, it would re-render the server component - this will re-fetch all the data on the page

  2. Lifting state up

    1. A parent component fetches the data and passes it on to child components

  3. Context API - only works for client components (hooks are used)

    1. A provider must be placed deep down in the component tree to avoid unnecessary re-renders

      1. Steps:

        1. A client component for context API needs to be craated
        "use client";

import { createContext, useContext, useState } from "react";

const ReservationContext = createContext();

const initialState = { from: undefined, to: undefined };

function ReservationProvider({ children }) {
  const [range, setRange] = useState(initialState);

  return (
    <ReservationContext.Provider value={{ range, setRange }}>
      {children}
    </ReservationContext.Provider>
  );
}

function useReservation() {
  const context = useContext(ReservationContext);

  if (context === undefined)
    throw new Error("Context was used outside provider");

  return context;
}

export { ReservationProvider, useReservation };

        1. Use the provider in the global layout.js

          1. We can pass server components as children to the provider
          <div className="flex-1 px-8 py-12 grid">
  <main className="max-w-7xl mx-auto w-full">
    <ReservationProvider>{children}</ReservationProvider>
  </main>
</div>

  4. If state changes, client component re-renders

    1. If a component re-renders, all its child components re-render

  5. Code: ReservationContext.js

"use client";

import { createContext, useContext, useState } from "react";

const ReservationContext = createContext();

const initialState = { from: undefined, to: undefined };

function ReservationProvider({ children }) {
  const [range, setRange] = useState(initialState);

  const resetRange = () => setRange(initialState);

  return (
    <ReservationContext.Provider value={{ range, setRange, resetRange }}>
      {children}
    </ReservationContext.Provider>
  );
}

function useReservation() {
  const context = useContext(ReservationContext);

  if (context === undefined)
    throw new Error("Context was used outside provider");

  return context;
}

export { ReservationProvider, useReservation };
  1. Code: ReservationReminder.js
"use client";

import { XMarkIcon } from "@heroicons/react/24/solid";
import { format } from "date-fns";
import { useReservation } from "./ReservationContext";

function ReservationReminder() {
  const { range, resetRange } = useReservation();

  if (!range.from || !range.to) return null;

  return (
    <div className="fixed bottom-6 left-1/2 -translate-x-1/2 py-5 px-8 rounded-full bg-accent-500 text-primary-800 text  font-semibold shadow-xl shadow-slate-900 flex gap-8 items-center">
      <p>
        <span>👋</span> Don&apos;t forget to reserve your dates <br /> from{" "}
        {format(new Date(range.from), "MMM dd yyyy")} to{" "}
        {format(new Date(range.to), "MMM dd yyyy")}
      </p>
      <button
        className="rounded-full p-1 hover:bg-accent-600 transition-all"
        onClick={resetRange}
      >
        <XMarkIcon className="h-5 w-5" />
      </button>
    </div>
  );
}

export default ReservationReminder;

Constructing an API Endpoint with Route Handlers

  1. API endpoint from Next.JS using Route Handlers
    1. Not as important as it was with pages router
      1. Now we use Server Actions for mutations
    2. API Endpoints can be constructed using Route handlers
      1. route.js - convention file
        1. It can be in any folder that doesn't have page.js yet
          1. Since no HTML is returned
            1. JSON data is returned
          2. The route handler is executed and returns JSON data
        2. It can have one or more functions each corresponding to HTTP verbs
        3. Response - Interface of Fetch API
          1. Response.json() - Returns a new Response object for returning the provided JSON encoded data
  2. Code: api/cabins/[cabinId]
import { getBookedDatesByCabinId, getCabin } from "@/app/_lib/data-service";

export async function GET(request, { params }) {
  const { cabinId } = params;

  try {
    const [cabin, bookedDates] = await Promise.all([
      getCabin(cabinId),
      getBookedDatesByCabinId(cabinId),
    ]);
    return Response.json({ cabin, bookedDates });
  } catch (e) {
    return Response.json({ message: "Cabin not found" });
  }

  console.log(request, params);

  return Response.json({ text: "text" }); // Response - web-standard also implemented in browsers. MDN documentation
}

import { auth } from "../_lib/auth";
import { getBookedDatesByCabinId, getSettings } from "../_lib/data-service";
import DateSelector from "./DateSelector";
import LoginMessage from "./LoginMessage";
import ReservationForm from "./ReservationForm";

async function Reservation({ cabin }) {
  const [settings, bookedDates] = await Promise.all([
    getSettings(cabin.id),
    getBookedDatesByCabinId(cabin.id),
  ]);

  const session = await auth();

  return (
    <div className="grid grid-cols-2 border border-primary-800 min-h-[400px]">
      <DateSelector
        settings={settings}
        bookedDates={bookedDates}
        cabin={cabin}
      />
      {session?.user ? (
        <ReservationForm cabin={cabin} user={session.user} />
      ) : (
        <LoginMessage />
      )}
    </div>
  );
}

export default Reservation;

What is Middleware in Next.js?

  1. Middleware
    1. It is a program that sits between two things
      1. Next.js - Middleware is a function that sits between the request and the response
        1. It allows us to run some code based on the request but before the response is completed
  2. Middleware Details
    1. By default, middleware runs before every route in a project, but we can specify which paths using a matcher
      1. It always runs after the request, but before the route the user is visiting is rendered and sent back
    2. Analogy: Chunk of code that's in every page.js component (route)
      1. It is in one central place that runs before routes
    3. Only one middleware function: needs to be exported from middleware.js (or .ts) in the project root folder (not app folder)
  3. Use Cases:
    1. Read and set cookies and headers
    2. Authentication and authorization
    3. Server-side analytics
    4. Redirect based on geolocation (for intnl)
    5. A/B testing
  4. Middleware needs to produce a response: Two ways
    1. Redirects or rewrites
    2. Send response directly (usually JSON) to client
      1. Route is bypassed
        1. Use-Case: Send data response as JSON like API (we have route handlers as alternatives)

Protecting Routes with NextAuth Middleware

  1. Code: middleware.js
import { NextResponse } from "next/server";

export function middleware(request) {
  console.log(request);

  return NextResponse.redirect(new URL("/about", request.url));
}

export const config = {
  matcher: ["/account", "/cabins"],
};

  1. auth also acts as a middleware

    1. Code: middleware.js
    import { auth } from "@/app/_lib/auth";
export const middleware = auth;

export const config = {
  matcher: ["/account"],
};

  2. Code: auth.js

import NextAuth from "next-auth";
import Google from "next-auth/providers/google";

const authConfig = {
  providers: [
    Google({
      clientId: process.env.AUTH_GOOGLE_ID,
      clientSecret: process.env.AUTH_GOOGLE_SECRET,
    }),
  ],
  callbacks: {
    authorized({ auth, request }) {
      return !!auth?.user;
    },
  },
};

export const {
  auth,
  handlers: { GET, POST },
} = NextAuth(authConfig);

Section 37: Mutations with Server Actions + Modern React Hooks

Section Overview

  1. Topics:
    1. Server actions for data mutations
      1. In RSCs
    2. Implementing features: Constructing, updating, and deleting a reservation + updating profile
    3. New cutting-edge React hooks
      1. For performance and interactivity
        1. useFormStatus
        2. useTransition
        3. useOptimistic

What are Server Actions?

  1. Server Actions:
    1. Goal: To build interactive full-stack applications with React
      1. Solution: RSC architecture
        1. data Fetching: Server Components (most fundamental part of RSC architecture)
        2. Mutations: Server Actions
    2. The missing piece in the RSC architecture that enables interactive full-stack applications (CRUD)
    3. Async functions that run exclusively on the server, allowing us to perform data mutations
    4. Created with the "use server" directive at the top of the function or an entire module
      1. An async function in a server component.
        1. Can be used in component or passed to a client component (unlike functions)
          1. Can be used directly in a server component OR
          2. Can be passed as a prop in a client component
            1. We cannot usually pass functions as props to client components
              1. Server actions is an exception
      2. Standalone file: exported functions (multiple server actions) become server actions that can be imported into any component [recommended]
        1. All mutations are stored in a central place (it is okay to have multiple files)
      3. "use server" is only for server actions (not for SCs)
        1. It bridges the gap from client to server (allowing client to talk to the server)
          1. Like an API call
    5. Behind the scenes: Next.js constructs an API endpoint (with URL (each its own URL)) for each server action. Whenever a server action is called, a POST request is made to its URL (the function itself never reaches client)
      1. The code stays always on the server
        1. It is safe to connect to DBs, use secret keys, ...
      2. When a server action is invoked, it gets translated to a POST request with data automatically serialized
        1. The function abstracts API endpoint details
    6. We don't have to manually construct APIs or route handlers to mutate data
    7. Unlike server components, server actions actually require a running web server
      1. SCs can run at build-time
      2. Web server is required for the endpoints
    8. Server Actions can be called from:
      1. action attribute in a <form> element (in server and client components)
        1. The form can be in SC or CC
          1. No additional code on the front-end
        2. It sends all data in a serialized form to the API endpoint behind the scenes
      2. Event handlers (only client components)
      3. useEffect (only client components)
    9. In Server Actions, we can:
      1. Perform data mutations (creation, updation, deletion)
      2. Update the UI with new data: Revalidate cache with revalidatePath and revalidateTag (manually on demand)
        1. SAs are tightly integrated with Next.js caching and revalidation system
      3. Work with cookies
      4. Many more... (almost any code)
    10. Note: We need to ensure that the user is authorized to perform the action and we must assume that inputs are unsafe
    11. SAs cannot be defined in CCs

Updating the Profile Using a Server Action

  1. form with action containing SA doesn't reload the page
  2. form also passes all form data using native formData API
  3. Code: actions.js
"use server";

import { auth, signIn, signOut } from "./auth";
import { supabase } from "./supabase";

export async function updateGuest(formData) {
  const session = await auth();

  if (!session) throw new Error("You must be logged in"); // caught by closest error boundary `Error.js`

  const nationalID = formData.get("nationalID");
  const [nationality, countryFlag] = formData.get("nationality").split("%");

  if (!/^[a-zA-Z0-9]{6,12}$/.test(nationalID))
    throw new Error("Please provide a valid national ID");

  const updateData = { nationality, countryFlag, nationalID };

  const { data, error } = await supabase
    .from("guests")
    .update(updateData)
    .eq("id", session.user.guestId);

  if (error) throw new Error("Guest could not be updated");
}

export async function signInAction() {
  await signIn("google", { redirectTo: "/account" });
}

export async function signOutAction() {
  await signOut({ redirectTo: "/" });
}
  1. Code: account/profile/page.js

        Update your guest profile
      </h2>

      </p>

}
